########## p-051.jpg ##########
             Student Conduct
             Institutional Rule 2 defines and governs what constitutes misconduct by students
             and outlines the procedures for addressing such misconduct. This rule is not a
             general civility code to overregulate student life. The free exchange of ideas and the
             pursuit of truth can sometimes be challenging and uncomfortable, and it is not the
             role of the University of Austin to police petty slights or hurt feelings. Students are
             adults and are expected to engage responsibly, recognizing that disagreement and
             difficult conversations are an integral part of the UATX experience.
             Instead, this policy aims to correct and, when appropriate, sanction misconduct
             that is harmful to campus life. Such violations can undermine our academic mission
             and negatively impact the physical safety and well-being of others.
             Mistakes are an inevitable part of growth. For most minor or first-time violations,
             informal resolution methods, such as coaching, expectation-setting, or written
             warnings, are the appropriate interventions. And when formal sanctions are
             necessary, they must be educational, proportional, and consistent with our values of
             growth and accountability. Suspension or expulsion should be reserved for the most
             serious or repeated cases.
             While Rule 2 governs student conduct matters generally, Institutional Rules 3 and 4
             provide the procedures for alleged acts of sexual misconduct and discrimination.
             These rules along with Rule 2 are attached to this catalog and may also be found in
             the Student Handbook and policy library on the website.
             Student Grievances
             You should contact University officials if you have a concern. If you're unsure
             about the right contact, please reach out to the Office of Students and Community
             for assistance.
                                                                                                      51
             THE UNIVERSITY OF AUSTIN                                      ACADEMIC CATALOG  .2026-2027


########## p-052.jpg ##########
             Academic Grievance
             For academic issues, schedule time directly with individual instructors to discuss
             any concerns or grievances about classes, grades, and instruction. Extensions of
             time, change in scope or modality granted for a student relating to a grievance shall
             be considered for all within the course. If unsatisfied, any student may submit a
             written grievance to the Dean or Director responsible for the course in question.
             General Misconduct by a Fellow Student
             If you have a serious grievance with a fellow student, please report it to the Dean
             of Students.
             Reporting Sexual Misconduct
             UATX encourages anyone who has experienced, witnessed, or knows of prohibited
             sexual misconduct to report it. Reports may be submitted via email
             (compliance@uaustin.org), to the Sexual Misconduct Coordinator
             (kespino@uaustin.org) in person or via email, or through the "Report a Concern"
             link on UATX's website: uaustin.org/legal or by using the QR code on the "Report a
             Concern" posters throughout campus.
             Reporting allows the University to connect individuals with support services, assess
             safety risks, and determine appropriate next steps. Anyone-regardless of whether
             they are directly involved in the incident—may submit a report. Anonymous reports
             are accepted, using the "Report a Concern" link on UATX's website or the QR code
             on posters throughout campus, though anonymity may limit the University's ability
             to take further action.
             An enrolled student is not required to report an incident of sexual harassment,
             sexual assault, dating violence, or stalking. A student who reports such an incident
             to UATX, a health care provider, a law enforcement agency, or another third party is
             entitled to accommodations as necessary. Please contact the SMRC to learn more
             about accommodations.

########## p-054.jpg ##########
             Student Discipline Records
             The University maintains a record for each student who has received disciplinary
             sanctions. These records include:
              • The nature of the violation
              • The sanction issued
              • Any supporting documentation
             Confidentiality
              • Discipline records are kept confidential in accordance with applicable laws.
              • Current academic status, including expulsions, are noted on the transcript.
              • Records may only be disclosed:
                 • With written student consent
                 • In response to a valid subpoena
              • Records are retained for seven years after the student's final term of enrollment.
             Preferred Name and Gender Identity
             The University of Austin does not require faculty, staff, or students to refer to others
             by preferred names or gender identities. All students are recorded under their legal
             name as submitted at enrollment. Official University documents, including
             transcripts, diplomas, and correspondence, will reflect the student's legal name. The
             University respects the dignity of every student, but does not mandate institutional
             or personal speech related to gender identity or preferred naming.

########## p-055.jpg ##########
             UATX Report a Concern
             UATX maintains an official compliance reporting portal. This online tool provides
             students, employees, and members of the public with a clear and consistent way to
             raise concerns about university operations, safety, ethics, or potential violations of
             university policy. Reports may be submitted at any time. If you have a concern,
             submit it. You are not expected to investigate the matter yourself. Each report is
             reviewed and directed to the appropriate office for response. Reports are handled
             discreetly, and information is shared only as needed to evaluate and address the
             issue. You may submit a report anonymously. Retaliation against any individual
             who makes a good-faith report violates university policy. If there is an immediate
             emergency or active threat, call 911 or contact Campus Security (512-516-4983).
             To submit a report, visit: uaustin.speakup.report/Compliance

########## p-056.jpg ##########
             SECTION 07
             Undergraduate Degree Requirements
             The UATX undergraduate curriculum is designed to immerse students in a
             community of teaching and learning that emphasizes academic rigor, civil
             discourse, and intellectual risk-taking. While the curriculum has more early
             structure than many, students will have opportunities to pursue their particular
             interests from the very beginning of their education at UATX.
             Bachelor of Arts in Liberal Studies
             Students must complete 180 credit hours (45 credit hours per academic year) to
             graduate with a Bachelor of Arts in Liberal Studies, normally distributed over three
             main terms per year with a course load of fifteen hours per term. Students must
             complete the undergraduate degree program within a five-year period, unless
             granted an extension by the Provost. All course listings that follow are 3 credit hours
             unless otherwise specified.
             Within these 180 credit hours, they must complete the following:
               Intellectual Foundations               57 credit hours
               Liberal Studies Major                  96 credit hours
               Polaris                                27 credit hours
               Total for degree                       180 credit hours
                                                                                                      56
             THE UNIVERSITY OF AUSTIN                                      ACADEMIC CATALOG • 2026-2027


########## p-057.jpg ##########
             Intellectual Foundations
             Thoughtful and ethical innovators, builders, leaders, and citizens must be literate,
             numerate, conversant with the varieties of knowledge, and adept at sorting signals
             from noise across multiple domains of human experience. Informed by a deep
             understanding of the conditions of human flourishing, they must appreciate the
             advantages and problems of modernity and the promise and challenge of the
             American experiment.
             Intellectual Foundations consists of courses that introduce students to the various
            languages of understanding and teach them to see things whole, make connections,
             and sort signals from noise across multiple domains of experience. All course
             listings that follow are 3 credit hours unless otherwise specified.
             The intended goals and outcomes of the Intellectual Foundations are as follows:
              • Display proficiency in the English language through interpretation of texts, persuasive
                writing, and effective speaking;
              • Model and solve problems using quantitative reasoning tools, and understand how to
                quantify uncertainty as it applies to decision-making and risk;
              • Achieve a critical understanding of the varieties of knowledge, their interrelationship,
                and the difference between knowledge and wisdom;
              • Identify the necessary conditions of flourishing human lives and communities;
              • Achieve a critical understanding of modern society and Western culture;
              • Understand and appreciate the unique vibrancy of the American form of government
                and way of life.
             Intellectual Foundations courses include the following required 57 credits:

########## p-059.jpg ##########
             Liberal Studies Major
             Program Overview and Objectives
             The goal of the Liberal Studies Major is to provide students with both rigorous
             disciplinary depth and interdisciplinary agility. The curriculum in the major guides
             students through specialized coursework, primary text analysis, and domain-
             specific methodologies, preparing them to synthesize knowledge and solve complex
             modern challenges.
             Program Student Learning Outcomes are:
              • Disciplinary Mastery & Methodological Application. Apply core theories,
                foundational principles, and domain-specific methodologies within a chosen field (or
                across fields) to evaluate complex problems.
              • Interdisciplinary Synthesis & Primary Source Analysis. Synthesize concepts,
                frameworks, and primary texts across multiple disciplines to construct well-reasoned,
                integrated perspectives on open questions.
             The Liberal Studies Major program shall consist of 96 credits outside of
             required Intellectual Foundations and Polaris courses, with the following
             additional requirements:
              • 200-Level Coursework: Minimum 18 credits
              • 300/400-Level Coursework: Minimum 45 credits
              • Electives: Remaining credits selected in consultation with a Faculty Advisor
             Centers of Inquiry
             UATX has two academic centers of inquiry responsible for delivering the majority of
             the courses contributing to the Intellectual Foundations and Liberal Arts Major
             components of the degree program: Arts and Sciences.
                                                                                                      59
             THE UNIVERSITY OF AUSTIN                                      ACADEMIC CATALOG  .2026-2027


########## p-060.jpg ##########
             Center For Arts
             The mission of the Center for Arts is to develop the intellectual skills and historical
             awareness necessary to make informed, lasting, and important contributions to
             human flourishing by studying and participating in debates about systems of belief,
             analyzing and creating works of art, and investigating the causes and effects of
             historical events.
             Degree: Bachelor of Arts • Major: Liberal Studies • Concentrations: Philosophy, History, Literature,
             American Civilization
             The intended learning outcomes for the Center for Arts are:
              • Understand and describe the history of Western civilization from antiquity to the present
                day, with reference to theology, philosophy, and the arts as well as material conditions,
                military conflicts, and political changes.
              • Recognize and explain causal connections between beliefs about religion, politics,
                ethics, and aesthetics and pivotal events in the history of Western civilization.
              • Discern and articulate the relation between form, content, and historical context in
                 works of art and debates about systems of belief.
             Center For Sciences
             The mission of the Center for Science is to teach students to think and reason
             quantitatively, exercise creativity, formulate and solve complex problems, and
             communicate technical solutions clearly in order to inform good decision-making
             and foster innovation.
             Degree: Bachelor of Arts • Major: Liberal Studies • Concentrations: Mathematics, Computer Science
             and Artificial Intelligence, Economics
             The Center for Science at UATX will be a community of exceptional scholars and
             purpose-driven students, deeply engaged with each other and with cooperating
             partners to cultivate technically-informed thinking and doing. The program
             curriculum combines best-in-practice academic content with applications and
            lessons learned from influential thinkers and doers in the private and public sectors.
                                                                                                      60
             THE UNIVERSITY OF AUSTIN                                      ACADEMIC CATALOG. 2026-2027


########## p-061.jpg ##########
             Upon completion of this program, students will be able to:
              • Speak, read, and write in mathematical language.
              • Formulate and solve quantitative problems using appropriate technical language,
                models, and techniques;
              • Collect and analyze data using appropriate technology tools;
              • Communicate technical solutions clearly through writing, speaking, and visualization.
             Academic Concentrations and Applied Tracks
             Each Academic Concentration and Applied Track consists of 36 credits, 18 at the
             200-level and 18 at the 300/400 level. Specific course requirements for each are listed
             below. The cognizant dean or director for each concentration or track may approve
             equivalencies and substitutions. All course listings that follow are 3 credit hours
             unless otherwise specified.
             Academic Concentration: Philosophy
             The Philosophy concentration trains students to read the primary texts of the
             Western tradition closely and to reason rigorously about the questions those texts
             raise, including what can be known, what is good, what is just, what is beautiful,
             and what it means to live well. Building on the Intellectual Foundations sequence,
             students work through a chronological survey of the tradition from the Hellenistic
             period to the twentieth century, then move into intensive seminars devoted to single
             philosophers, movements, and problems. Offerings range across theology,
             metaphysics, epistemology, ethics, and politics. The concentration is broad by
             design, incorporating analytic philosophy alongside continental and cross-listing
             widely with History, Literature, and American Civilization so that students
             encounter philosophical arguments in their original historical and literary settings.
             Upon completion of the Philosophy concentration, students will be able to:
                                                                                                      61l
             THE UNIVERSITY OF AUSTIN                                      ACADEMIC CATALOG. 2026-2027


########## p-062.jpg ##########
              • Interpret primary philosophical texts: Read demanding primary sources closely and
                reconstruct their arguments in the author's own terms, attending to technical
                vocabulary, literary form, and historical context.
              • Construct and evaluate philosophical arguments: Analyze, defend, and criticize
                positions in writing and in discussion by identifying premises and assumptions,
                assessing inferences, and anticipating serious objections.
              • Trace the development of the tradition: Situate figures, schools, and problems within
                the arc of philosophy from antiquity to the present, explaining how later thinkers
                inherit, revise, or repudiate the work of their predecessors.
              • Bring philosophical inquiry to bear on enduring and contemporary questions:
                Apply the resources of the tradition to live problems in ethics, politics, religion, art,
                science, and technology, and articulate reasoned positions on them.
             Lower Division Philosophy Concentration Courses - Complete the following 5 courses:

             PHIL 220 Early Modern Philosophy

             LITR 220 Milton

########## p-064.jpg ##########
             Academic Concentration: History
             The History concentration examines how civilizations emerge, flourish, and
             decline, equipping students to understand the ideas, institutions, events, conflicts,
             and personalities that have shaped the modern world. Highlighting Western
             civilization within the context of world history, the History concentration delivers a
             broad and deep understanding of the human past and how it relates to politics, law,
             religion, ethics, the arts, and other domains of thought. After completing surveys
             covering antiquity, the Middle Ages, the Renaissance, early modernity, and the
             modern world from the nineteenth century to the present, students pursue eighteen
             credits of advanced coursework in areas of particular interest.
             Upon completion of the History Concentration, students will be able to:
              • Demonstrate a broad understanding of Western history from the first marks of human
                civilization to the present, including the most important world-historical systems of
                belief, institutions, technologies, conflicts, and events.
              • Analyze historical evidence, evaluate competing interpretations, and construct
                persuasive historical arguments applicable to intellectual, political, legal, and
                commercial contexts.
              • Construct and communicate historically informed arguments, supported by evidence, in
                 written and oral forms for academic, professional, and public audiences with precision,
                clarity, and intellectual integrity.
             Lower Division History Concentration Courses — Complete the following 3 courses:
             HIST 211 Renaissance and Reformation
             HIST 215 Romanticism and Realism
             HIST 220 Decadence, Modernism, and the Great War
             Complete 3 courses from the following:

########## p-066.jpg ##########
             Academic Concentration: Literature
             The Literature Concentration introduces students to major works, authors, genres,
             and traditions of Western literature while developing skills in close reading, critical
             argument, and creative writing. Students explore how literature gives form to
             human experience and learn to understand literary works through both
             interpretation and the practice of writing. After completing a historical sequence,
             students pursue advanced study of particular periods, authors, genres, and
             approaches to literature. The concentration teaches students to read closely, write
             with purpose, and understand the power of language and literary form.
             Upon completion of the Literature concentration, students will be able to:
              • Interpret literary texts: read works of poetry, drama, fiction, and literary nonfiction
                closely, analyzing how language, form, genre, style, and structure contribute to literary
                meaning and effect.
              • Situate literature in historical and intellectual context: explain how major literary
                works and traditions engage the political, religious, philosophical, and cultural questions
                of their periods, tracing significant developments across literary history.
              • Synthesize literary traditions and ideas: compare works across authors, genres,
                periods, and traditions in order to identify continuities, transformations, and significant
                questions in the history of literature.
              • Develop and evaluate literary arguments: formulate persuasive interpretations of
                literary texts, support those interpretations with textual evidence, engage critically with
                competing readings and works of literary criticism, and communicate arguments clearly
                in writing and discussion.
             Lower Division Literature Concentration Courses - Complete the following 5 courses:
             LITR 210 Medieval Literature                    LITR 225 Eighteenth-Century Literature
             LITR 215 Shakespeare                            LITR 230 Literary Criticism
             LITR 220 Milton
             Complete 1 course from the following:

########## p-068.jpg ##########
             Academic Concentration: American Civilization
             The Concentration in American Civilization invites students into an
             interdisciplinary study of America through an examination of the ideals that define
             our national aims, the social, political, and religious institutions that give those
             ideals concrete form, and the rhetoric, literature, art, and stories that cultivate
             attachment to our nation and its distinctive history. Students progress from broad
             interdisciplinary survey courses to intensive seminars on the thinkers, statesmen,
             debates, historical events, and works of art that have made America what it is: a
             distinctive capstone of Western civilization.
              • Explain the fundamental ideals of the American tradition, including liberty, equality,
                self-government, constitutionalism, natural rights, federalism, and the common good,
                and situate those ideals within the broader inheritance of Western civilization.
              • Analyze the historical development and proper function of American institutions,
                including the social, political, and religious institutions through which the nation's
                ideals have been embodied, preserved, and transmitted across generations.
              • Interpret major works of American culture, including rhetoric, sermons, art, literature,
                 music, and cinema, as expressions of America's distinctive character and as means by
                which Americans have cultivated attachment to common life.
              • Apply knowledge of American ideals, institutions, and culture to the responsibilities of
                contemporary civic life, through the exercise of mature judgment, participation in civil
                discourse, and the cultivation of the virtues and habits necessary for self-government.
             Lower Division American Civilization Concentration Courses — Complete the following 5 courses:
             AMCV 210 American History I: Colonial Period to Civil War
             AMCV 215 American Literature I: Eighteenth and Nineteenth Centuries
             AMCV 220 American Politics I: Ideas and Institutions

             Academic Concentration: Mathematics
             The Mathematics concentration bridges theoretical rigor and practical
             computational skills. After completing core coursework in mathematical reasoning,
             linear algebra, probability, and data science, students customize 18 upper-division
             credits in pure, applied, or statistical mathematics.
             Upon completion of the Mathematics concentration, students will be able to:
              • Construct and evaluate formal proofs: Formulate, write, and analyze mathematically
                rigorous proofs using deductive logic, direct and indirect proof techniques, and
                foundational principles across pure and applied mathematics.
              • Apply mathematical and computational modeling: Implement mathematical
                methods, statistical techniques, and algorithms—utilizing tools from data science,
                linear algebra, and differential equations—to model, analyze, and solve complex real-
                world problems.
              • Analyze advanced quantitative concepts: Synthesize concepts from upper-level
                coursework (such as real/complex analysis, modern algebra, numerical methods, or
                advanced statistics) to evaluate theoretical frameworks and tackle domain-specific
                quantitative challenges.
             Lower Division Mathematics Concentration Courses - Pre-requisite foundational credits:

             Academic Concentration: Computer
             Science and Artificial Intelligence
             The Computer Science and Artificial Intelligence (CSAI) concentration prepares
             students for entrepreneurship, professional work, or graduate study in Computer
             Science, with a focus on traditional concepts along with modern AI-enabled
             development of software. Students in this concentration may also choose a focus in
             current AI, with a curriculum covering both the mathematical theory and practical
             applications of deep learning.
             Upon completing the CSAI concentration, students will:
              • Understand fundamentals of Computer Science and software development, with further
                focus on one of these two areas:
                 • Design and construction of software systems using modern Al tools
                 • Theoretical and practical understanding of deep learning AI
              • Be prepared for effective, ethical contribution to computing in an entrepreneurial,
                professional, and/or research capacity.

             The Economics concentration teaches core tools of economic theory and
             quantitative analysis and then applies them to study a wide range of economic
             topics including monetary and fiscal policy, economic growth, financial markets
             and international trade and finance.
             Upon completing the Economics concentration, students will:
              • Evaluate complex economic arguments as presented in the press and policy debates.
              • Understand the tools and approaches used to analyze economic data.
              • Participate as citizens in the evaluation of economic policies.

########## p-076.jpg ##########
             Polaris Project
             All UATX undergraduates are required to develop and complete a Polaris Project.
             Rather than serving as a conventional thesis or final-year capstone, Polaris is
             integrated throughout the undergraduate experience. Students begin by identifying
             a problem, opportunity, or question worthy of sustained commitment over several
             years. Polaris gives students dedicated time to execute, test, and refine their ideas
             beyond the classroom. Like Polaris, the North Star used by seafarers to navigate for
             millennia, the project represents a distant objective toward which the student's
             efforts are directed.
             For a detailed project overview and objectives, please see the Polaris Guidebook and
             Standards document.
             Project Overview and Objectives
              • Pursue ambitious, self-directed work over an extended period.
              • Plan, execute, and publicly release a completed project that demonstrates
                objective benchmarks.
             The Polaris Curriculum
             27 Polaris credits are required to complete the Polaris Project. Most credits are
             earned through the "Polaris Build" class in which students work on their projects. A
             minimum of 21 credit hours of Polaris Build is required. Of those 21, up to 6 credits
             can be "Build equivalents." Build equivalents include:
              • Polaris courses, bootcamps, and exams that may serve as electives or as requirements for
                particular types of projects
              • approved experiences-such as internships and apprenticeships
             Exceptions to the 6-credit-hour maximum for Build Equivalents require the
             permission of the Executive Director of the Innovation Labs. Further detail can be
             found in the Polaris Guidebook and Standards.
                                                                                                      76
             THE UNIVERSITY OF AUSTIN                                      ACADEMIC CATALOG. 2026-2027

########## p-078.jpg ##########
             SECTION 08
             Course Descriptions
             2026-2027 Catalog Courses
             AMCV
             AMCV 110 The American Century                                                           3cr
             This course explores the political, religious, economic, intellectual, and cultural forces that shaped
             America from World War I through the Cold War. Students engage directly with primary sources as
             they study World War I, the Roaring Twenties, the Great Depression, World War II, the Civil Rights
             Era, and the Cold War. As the United States weathered new challenges including modernism,
             economic upheaval, profound social change, the rise of consumer culture, and its own emergence as a
             global power, how did Americans work to preserve constitutional self-government? Through careful
             examination of political and religious writings, public oratory, historical documents, literature, music,
             film, and visual art, students reflect on the relationship between domestic and foreign affairs and the
             ways Americans expressed and defended the nation's ideals, institutions, and culture while assuming
             the responsibilities of global leadership.
             AMCV 200 The American Founding                                                          3cr
             This course examines the intellectual, political, religious, and cultural origins of the American
             republic from the colonial period through the Founding. Students engage directly with primary
             sources and in particular The Federalist Papers as they study the principles, institutions, and debates
             that emerged in Puritan New England, developed during the colonial era, and culminated in the
             American Revolution and the ratification of the U.S. Constitution. Through careful examination of
             political and religious writings as well as historical documents, literature, and visual art students
             consider the complex relationship between ideals, institutions, and culture that produced one of
             history's most distinctive and enduring achievements in constitutional self-government.
                                                                                                      78
             THE UNIVERSITY OF AUSTIN                                      ACADEMIC CATALOG • 2026-2027


########## p-079.jpg ##########
             AMCV 201 American Frontiers                                                             3 cr
             This course examines the expansion of the American republic across its geographic, political,
             religious, intellectual, and cultural frontiers during the nineteenth century. Students engage directly
             with primary sources as they study how America's government and broader way of life developed
             through the early republic, the antebellum era, the Civil War, Reconstruction, and the rise of
             industrial society. Through careful examination of philosophical and religious writings, political
             oratory, historical documents, poetry, short stories, and visual art, students consider how Americans
             confronted the challenges of democratization, slavery, civil war, industrialization, immigration,
             and social reform. Readings include Alexis de Tocqueville's Democracy in America, which serves
             as a guide for understanding the ideals, institutions, and culture that shaped America's distinctive
             national character.
             AMCV 210 American History I: Colonial Period to Civil War                               3 cr
             This course traces the historical development of the United States from the colonial period through
             the Civil War. Integrating intellectual, political, religious, and military history, students examine the
             ideas, institutions, leaders, conflicts, and cultural shifts that shaped the early American republic.
             Particular attention is placed on how the nation's ideals come to be embodied in social and political
             institutions and tested through war, reform, and constitutional crises.
             Prerequisite: AMCV 200 or INF 2121
             AMCV 215 American Literature I: Eighteenth and Nineteenth Centuries                     3 cr
             This course explores significant works of American poetry and fiction, along with notable literary
             essays, from the late-eighteenth and nineteenth centuries. Along the way, students consider the
             relationship between American literature and the broader social, cultural, and political
             transformations taking place in the developing nation. Writers may include Ralph Waldo Emerson,
             Henry David Thoreau, Walt Whitman, Nathaniel Hawthorne, Emily Dickinson, Herman Melville,
             Mark Twain, Edith Wharton, and Henry James.
             Prerequisite: AMCV 201 or INF 2200
                                                                                                      79
             THE UNIVERSITY OF AUSTIN                                      ACADEMIC CATALOG • 2026-2027


########## p-080.jpg ##########
             AMCV 220 American Politics I: Ideas and Institutions                                    3cr
             This course serves as an introduction to the principles and structures of American government.
             Students consider competing accounts of the philosophical foundations of the American regime
             alongside the historical development of major institutions such as Congress, the presidency, the
             judiciary, federalism, political parties, and the media. By comparing the Founders' aspirations with
             the present-day functioning of these institutions, the course explores the relationship between
             American political ideals, its governing institutions, and the realities of contemporary political life.
             AMCV 225 Tocqueville, Democracy in America                                              3cr
             This course offers a close reading of Alexis de Tocqueville's Democracy in America: the quintessential
             book for understanding the nature of both democracy and America. Students examine Tocqueville's
             reflections on liberty, equality, religion, local self-government, civic associations, the family, and the
             "habits of the heart" that sustain democratic life. By exploring the interplay of ideals, institutions, and
             culture in Tocqueville's account of America, the course establishes the conceptual framework that
             informs the study of American civilization.
             Prerequisite: AMCV 200 or INF 2121
             AMCV 230 American Philosophy: From Puritans to Pragmatists                              3 cr
             This course surveys the development of America's distinctive brand of philosophical thought from the
             colonial period through the rise of pragmatism. This course follows a broad intellectual trajectory in
             which religion, moral philosophy, practical experience, and democratic life form a distinctively
             American approach to truth, freedom, equality, and human flourishing. Authors likely to be included
             are Jonathan Edwards, Benjamin Franklin, John Adams, Ralph Waldo Emerson, Charles Peirce,
             William James, John Dewey, and George Santayana.
             AMCV 280 Special Topic in American Civilization                                        1.5 cr
            This course offers intensive study of a single topic or text in American civilization selected by the
             instructor. Content varies by offering.
             AMCV 281 Independent Study in American Civilization                                    1.5cr
             This course provides for individualized study of a topic in American civilization under the supervision
             of a faculty member, with content and requirements to be determined in consultation between
             the student and instructor. Permission of instructor, Academic Concentration Lead, and Dean of
             Arts required.
                                                                                                      80
             THE UNIVERSITY OF AUSTIN                                      ACADEMIC CATALOG. 2026-2027

             This course provides for individualized study of a topic in American civilization under the supervision

             the student and instructor. Permission of instructor, Academic Concentration Lead, and Dean of
             Arts required.
             AMCV 310 American History II: Civil War to Cold War                                     3 cr
             This course continues the historical development of the United States from the Civil War to the Cold
             War. Integrating intellectual, political, religious, and military history, students examine the ideas,
             institutions, leaders, conflicts, and cultural shifts that shaped America in the late nineteenth and
             twentieth centuries. Particular attention is given to how the nation's ideals, institutions, and culture
             were maintained, challenged, or transformed by war, economic change, reform movements, and the
            United States' emergence as a global power.
             Prerequisite: AMCV 210
                                                                                                      81
             THE UNIVERSITY OF AUSTIN                                      ACADEMIC CATALOG : 2026-2027


########## p-082.jpg ##########
             AMCV 315 American Literature II: Twentieth and Twenty-First Century                     3 cr
             This course continues students' exploration of American poetry, fiction, and essay writing in relation
             to significant political, social, and cultural developments in the twentieth and twenty-first centuries.
             Writers may include poets such as Eliot, Frost, and Stevens; novels and short fiction by Hemingway,
             Faulkner, Ellison, O'Connor, Steinbeck, Pynchon, Percy, McCarthy, and Robinson; and essays by
             Mencken, Baldwin, Didion, Thompson, and Foster Wallace, among others.
             Prerequisite: AMCV 110
             AMCV 320 American Politics II: Rights and Duties                                        3 cr
             This course examines the rights and duties that define the relationship between American citizens and
             their government. Organized around the U.S. Bill of Rights and other major constitutional
             amendments, students explore the development of competing contemporary interpretations of
             freedoms including speech, religion, press, assembly, due process, equal protection, and the right to
             bear arms. Through landmark judicial decisions and notable political controversies, this course
             prompts students to consider how the American constitutional system has sought to secure liberty
             while balancing the competing demands of the common good and public order.
             Prerequisite: AMCV 220
             AMCV 325 The U.S. Presidency                                                            3 crl
             This course examines both the historical and constitutional development of the U.S. presidency from
             the Founding to the present. Particular attention will be paid to the question of what constitutes
             Presidential greatness and its relationship to broader ideals of constitutional self-government.
             Students consider the domestic, administrative, and foreign policy elements of the office from a
             historical and biographical perspective and will be introduced to major theoretical frameworks for
             understanding the U.S. Presidency.
             Prerequisite: AMCV 310
             AMCV 330   U.S. Foreign Policy                                                          3 cr
             This course studies the historical development of American foreign policy from the Founding to the
             present. Students consider the ideas, institutions, leaders, conflicts, and strategic decisions that have
             shaped America's role in the world, considering enduring questions of national interest, sovereignty,
             security, commerce, and war. Through a study of major episodes in foreign policy history, the course
             examines how statesmen have sought to preserve the nation's constitutional order, protect its citizens,
             and advance American ideals while prudently navigating the demands of international politics.
             Prerequisite: AMCV 315
                                                                                                      82
             THE UNIVERSITY OF AUSTIN                                      ACADEMIC CATALOG. 2026-2027


########## p-083.jpg ##########
             AMCV 340 Religion and Politics in America                                               3 cr
             This course studies the historical development of religion in the United States, especially the major
             denominations of Christianity, and its influence on American political and civic life. Integrating
             religious history and sociology of religion, students explore the beliefs, practices, institutions,
             denominations, revivals, and cultural transformations that have shaped the American experience
             from the colonial period to the present. Particular attention is given to the ways religious communities
             have influenced America's political ideals, public institutions, and civic culture, as well as to the
             changing place of religion in an increasingly pluralistic society.
             Prerequisite: AMCV 201 or INF 2200
             AMCV 345 The Legacy of Slavery                                                          3 cr
             The "legacy of slavery" is a phrase we have all heard in history classrooms, on cable news, and even in
             presidential speeches. But what does it mean? And why does it matter? For the purposes of this class,
             we will define the "legacy of slavery" as the long-term consequences of American slavery that can still
             be seen, felt, or measured today. This definition may not be controversial. The controversy comes
             when people claim that specific features of modern American society fall under this definition. For
             instance, is America's national wealth the result of slavery? How about mass incarceration or racial
             inequality? Students will become acquainted with two broad schools of thought on the legacy of
             slavery. The maximalist school sees the fingerprints of slavery in many aspects of American society,
             while the minimalist school argues the opposite: America's modern problems have little to do with the
             legacy of slavery.
             Prerequisite: AMCV 201 or INF 2200
             AMCV 350 American Political Philosophy after WWI                                        3 cr
             This course examines the major currents of political philosophy that have shaped American
             intellectual and political life since the First World War. Through the writings of thinkers such as
             Walter Lippmann, Leo Strauss, Hannah Arendt, James Burnham, Eric Voegelin, Richard Weaver,
             Russell Kirk, John Rawls, Robert Nozick, and Alasdair MacIntyre, students will consider questions
             concerning modernity, justice, liberty, the state, American exceptionalism, and the common
             good. Particular attention is given to the HIST 130 or INF 2121 main streams of thought in post-
             WWI America.
                                                                                                      83
             THE UNIVERSITY OF AUSTIN                                      ACADEMIC CATALOG . 2026-2027


########## p-084.jpg ##########
             AMCV 355 Liberalism and Conservatism                                                    3cr
             This course examines the rise, crises, and competing responses of liberalism and conservatism in the
             modern West. Beginning with the Enlightenment project and its critics, we will trace the development
             of liberal reason, the socialist challenge, and the emergence of modern conservatism. Who governs?
             To what ends? By what standards of freedom, equality, or virtue? Instead of relying on today's
             partisan categories, this course explores these questions through the eyes of the thinkers who first
             articulated and contested the principles of modern politics.
             Prerequisite: HIST 210 or INF 2121
             AMCV 360   Political Theology                                                           3cr
             How does political philosophy relate to theology? Is the present-day crisis of liberalism a consequence
             of secularization? This course introduces students to key concepts of political theology through works
             by authors such as Augustine, Carl Schmitt, Leo Strauss, Eric Voegelin, John Rawls, Francis
             Fukuyama, Samuel Huntington, Charles Taylor, Patrick Deneen, and Peter Thiel.
             Prerequisite: HIST 130 or INF 2121
             AMCV 365   Philosophers of Political Economy                                            3 cr
             This course reviews the history of the development of economic and political ideas, with special
             emphasis on contributions from 1700 to 1950.
             Prerequisite: HIST 210 or INF 2121
             AMCV 370 Capitalism and its Critics                                                     3 cr
             How can we evaluate competing claims about capitalism and its alternatives? This course considers
             socialist and other criticisms of economies in which market outcomes and private ownership
             determine the allocation of resources, drawing on empirical evidence as well as philosophical
             cross-examination. What evidence do we have about the growth and distribution consequences of
             capitalist development?
             Prerequisite: HIST 111 or INF 2300
                                                                                                      84
             THE UNIVERSITY OF AUSTIN                                      ACADEMIC CATALOG : 2026-2027


########## p-085.jpg ##########
             AMCV 375 Statesmanship and American Greatness                                           3cr
             This course investigates the nature of political greatness through the lives, speeches, and writings of
             exceptional statesmen from the classical world to the present, with particular emphasis on the
             American tradition. Students consider the virtues and character of great leaders who preserved,
             defended, or reformed free political communities during moments of extraordinary crisis. Particular
             attention is given to the historical influence of great statesmen on America's political ideals,
             constitutional structure, and civic culture, as well as to the enduring question of what distinguishes
             genuine statesmanship from the mere pursuit of power.
             Prerequisite: AMCV 201 or INF 2200
             AMCV 380 Special Topic in American Civilization                                        1.5 cr
             This course offers intensive study of a single topic or text in American civilization selected by the
             instructor. Content varies by offering.

             AMCV 410 Oratory and Rhetoric                                                           3 cr
             This course examines rhetoric and public persuasion from the Greco-Roman world and its reception
             in the American republic. Students study foundational texts of classical rhetoric, alongside the
             speeches, sermons, and debates that have shaped American civic life. Particular attention is given to
             how American statesmen and public leaders adapted the classical tradition of rhetoric to articulate
             and defend the nation's ideals, institutions, and culture. Readings may include works by Isocrates,
             Lysias, Demosthenes, Aristotle, Cicero, and Quintilian together with speeches and writings by George
             Washington, Patrick Henry, John Adams, Noah Webster, Abraham Lincoln, Frederick Douglass,
             Calvin Coolidge, Martin Luther King, Jr., and other influential American orators.
             Prerequisite: AMCV 201 or INF 2200
             AMCV 415 Great Events of American History                                               3 cr
             This seminar offers intensive study of a major event or series of events in American history selected by
             the instructor. Topics and texts vary by offering.

             This seminar offers intensive study of a single major American intellectual, set of intellectuals, or
             school of thought selected by the instructor. Topics and texts vary by offering.
             AMCV 425 The American Novel                                                             3 cr
             This seminar offers intensive study of one or two major American novels selected by the instructor.
             Topics and texts vary by offering.
             AMCV 430 American Cinema                                                                3 cr
             This seminar offers intensive study of major American films selected by the instructor. Topics and
             texts vary by offering.

             scenarios. Topics include cryptography and public-key infrastructure, authentication and access
             control, malware analysis, intrusion detection and prevention systems, secure software development
             practices, penetration testing methodologies, and legal and ethical considerations in cybersecurity.

             ECON 101 Applied Issues and Perspectives in Economics I                                 3 cr
             This is an introductory course in Economics based upon current Economic issues such as the role of
             markets, the determinants and consequences of inequality and market interventions such as the
             minimum wage. It uses tools of Economics to define and analyze the debate around these issues.
             ECON 102 Applied Issues and Perspectives in Economics II                                3 cr
             This is an introductory course in Economics based upon current Economic issues with a focus on
             growth, alternative Economic systems and policy interventions. It uses tools of Economics to define
             and analyze the debate around these issues.

########## p-098.jpg ##########
             ECON 111 Accelerated Applied Economics |                                                3 cr
             This course is a version of ECON 101 intended for those concentrating in Economics.
             ECON 112 Accelerated Applied Economics II                                               3 cr
             This course is a version of ECON 102 intended for those concentrating in Economics.
             ECON 201 Microeconomic Theory I                                                         3 cr
             This course in microEconomic theory covers household and firm choice. It uses mathematical tools of
             constrained optimization.
             Prerequisite: ECON 101, ECON 102
             ECON 202 Macroeconomic Theory I                                                         3cr
             This is an advanced course in macroeconomics. It covers dynamic choices by households and firms. It
             introduces dynamic general equilibrium theory as a framework for analysis.
             Prerequisite: ECON 101, ECON 102
             ECON 203 Microeconomic Theory !                                                         3 cr
             This advanced course in microeconomics covers advanced topics such as the fundamental welfare
             theorems. The course covers deviations from perfect competition, such as market power and
             externalities, to consider the inefficiencies they can create, and the regulatory policies that have been
             developed to mitigate or prevent these deviations.
             Prerequisite: ECON 201
             ECON 204 Macroeconomic Theory II                                                        3 cr
             This is an advanced course in macroeconomics. This course uses dynamic equilibrium analysis
             to study fiscal and monetary policy. It also introduces the search theory approach to money and
             labor markets.
             Prerequisite: ECON 202
             ECON 220   Probability and Statistics                                                   3 cr
             This course familiarizes students with the essential probability theory and statistical tools Economists
             use to make inferences and decisions using data. A wide range of topics in probability, statistics and
             regression analysis are explored.

             THE UNIVERSITY OF AUSTIN                                      ACADEMIC CATALOG. 2026-2027


########## p-099.jpg ##########
             ECON 230 Mathematical Economics                                                        1.5 cr
             This course builds the foundational quantitative skills required for further study in Economics.
             ECON 251 Principles of Microeconomics |                                                 3 cr
             This course will develop students' understanding about a broad range of problems related to the
             operation of markets, social policy, and business. It studies the determinants of household demand,
             firm supply and market equilibrium.
             ECON 252 Principles of Macroeconomics |                                                 3 cr
             This course sequence will build an analytical macroeconomic framework and then use it to explain
             changing conditions in the world Economy and the challenges these present for modern policy
             makers and business leaders. Students will become familiar with the phases of the business cycle,
             monetary policy, economic growth, inflation, unemployment and fiscal policy.
             ECON 271 History of Economic Thought                                                    3 cr
             This course traces the development of Economic ideas from classical thinkers like Adam Smith and
             David Ricardo through Keynes and the modern era, showing how each generation's theories
             responded to the pressing questions of its time. Students learn how competing schools of thought-
             from classical and Marxist to Keynesian and monetarist—continue to shape debates over markets,
             government, and prosperity today.
             ECON 272 Economic History                                                               3 cr
             Economic History examines how Economies have evolved over time, using historical episodes— from
             the Industrial Revolution to the Great Depression—to illustrate core Economic principles in action.
             Students learn to interpret long-run patterns in growth, trade, and living standards, and to draw
            lessons from the past for understanding present-day Economic challenges.
             ECON 280 Special Topic in Economics                                                    1.5 cr

             ECON 290   Political Economy                                                            3 cr
             This course uses Economic reasoning to understand the world's most consequential policy challenges
             and other weird phenomena. From foreign aid to law and order under anarchy; from corruption to
             occupational licensing. We focus on tracing incentives and interests, winners and losers, unintended
             consequences across history, institutions, and borders. Topics shift each semester as we explore
             new terrain.
             Prerequisite: ECON 101

             Prerequisite: ECON 230

             THE UNIVERSITY OF AUSTIN                                      ACADEMIC CATALOG• 2026-2027


########## p-101.jpg ##########
             ECON 306 Economic Growth                                                                3 cr
             This course studies the relationship between human capital attainment and (endogenous) growth, as
             well as the historical causes and sources of Economic development. The course generally asks why
             some countries have experienced growth spurts or growth miracles while others have not. In
             particular we focus on the vast theoretical literature that posits that "institutions" (whatever that
             means) "matter" for development. Much of the course will be spent debating what "institutions
             matter" actually means, contextualizing such a conjecture by using mathematical models to discipline
             our analysis.
             Prerequisite: ECON 230
             ECON 332 Behavioral and Experimental Economics                                          3 cr
             Experimental and Behavioral Economics examines how real people actually make decisions, often
             departing from the rational, self-interested behavior assumed in standard Economic models. Students
             explore evidence from laboratory experiments and psychological research on topics like fairness, risk,
             and bias, and consider how these insights reshape Economic theory and policy.
             Prerequisite: ECON 230
             ECON 340 Fiscal Policy                                                                  3 cr
             This course uses theory and empirical tools to study topics related to fiscal policy. Topics will include
             optimal taxation, the provision of public goods, the dynamics of deficits and debt and social security.
             Prerequisite: ECON 230
             ECON 351 International Trade and Finance                                                3 cr
             This course studies why countries trade and considers the consequences for welfare of trade from
             theoretical and empirical perspectives, both in the past and the present. This course also analyses the
             role of international finance in allocating global resources through various financial instruments,
             including international capital flows associated with investments in debt, equity, and foreign-
             direct investment.
             Prerequisite: ECON 230
                                                                                                     101
             THE UNIVERSITY OF AUSTIN                                      ACADEMIC CATALOG : 2026-2027


########## p-102.jpg ##########
             ECON 353 Comparative Economic Systems                                                   3 cr
             Comparative Economic Systems examines how different Economies—from market-based to centrally
             planned, and the many hybrids in between—organize production, allocate resources, and distribute
             income. Students compare the historical performance of these systems, using real-world cases to
             understand the trade-offs between markets, government, and different approaches to property
             and coordination.
             Prerequisite: ECON 230
             ECON 360 Corporate Finance, Accounting, and Business Planning                           3 cr
             This course teaches students how to construct business plans and use them to formulate cash flow
             analysis from income and expense statements and balance sheets, and then use the cash flow analysis
             as a basis for financial planning, valuation of the business, and creating a strategy to fund the idea.
             Prerequisite: ECON 230
             ECON 361 Entrepreneurshi p and Entrepreneurial Finance                                  3 cr
             This course analyzes how entrepreneurs originate and fund business ideas, with special emphasis on
             understanding the role of venture capital in business formation and growth.
             Prerequisite: ECON 230
             ECON 362 Household Finance                                                              3 cr
             This course studies household choices regarding consumption, saving, and portfolio choice. A basic
             model of household optimal behavior is formulated and then used as a framework for interpreting
             financial outcomes. The course combines theory and evidence.
             Prerequisite: ECON 230
             ECON 370 Monetary Economics and Policy                                                  3 cr
             This course studies the demand for money in general equilibrium models, including search and
             matching. It uses these models to characterize optimal monetary policy. The course also presents
             evidence on the effects of monetary policy.
             Prerequisite: ECON 230
                                                                                                     102
             THE UNIVERSITY OF AUSTIN                                      ACADEMIC CATALOG : 2026-2027

             financial systems, and their relevance for the Economy.
             Prerequisite: ECON 230
             ECON 372 Crypto Currencies                                                              3 cr

             Prerequisite: ECON 230
             ECON 373   Financial Crises                                                             3 cr
             This course explores the causes of and policy responses to financial crises in the United States before
             and since the formation of the Federal Reserve with emphasis on the Great Depression and the Great
             Recession of 2007-2008.
             Prerequisite: ECON 230

########## p-104.jpg ##########
             ECON 390 Labor Economics                                                                3 cr
             Labor Economics studies how wages, employment, and working conditions are determined through
             the interaction of workers and firms in the labor market. Students examine topics like human capital,
             discrimination, unions, and unemployment, and consider how government policies shape labor
             market outcomes.
             Prerequisite: ECON 230
             ECON 391 Economics of Education                                                         3 cr
             Economics of Education examines how individuals and societies invest in human capital through
             schooling, and how education affects earnings, productivity, and economic growth. Students explore
             topics like the returns to education, school choice, and the role of government in financing and
             regulating education systems. Important topics will include mismatch and the role of human capital
             accumulation for Economic growth.
             Prerequisite: ECON 230
             ECON 392 Independent Study in Economics                                                4.5 cr
             Independent study
             ECON 396 Law and Economics                                                              3 cr
             Law and Economics applies Economic reasoning to the analysis of legal rules and institutions,
             examining how laws governing property, contracts, and torts shape incentives and behavior. Students
             learn to evaluate legal rules in terms of efficiency and consider how the law can be designed to
             promote better Economic outcomes.
             Prerequisite: ECON 230
             ECON 401 Advanced Topics in Microeconomics                                             1.5 cr
             This course covers topics in microeconomics intended to prepare a student for advanced studies.
             Prerequisite: ECON 230
             ECON 402 •Advanced Topics in Macroeconomics                                            1.5 cr
             This course covers topics in macroeconomics intended to prepare a student for advanced studies. May
             be repeated up to 3 times.
             Prerequisite: ECON 230

             THE UNIVERSITY OF AUSTIN                                      ACADEMIC CATALOG • 2026-2027


########## p-105.jpg ##########
             ECON 405 Game Theory                                                                    3cr
             Game Theory introduces the tools economists use to analyze strategic interactions, where the
             outcome for each person depends not just on their own choices but on the choices of others. Students
             learn key concepts like Nash equilibrium and apply them to real-world situations ranging from
             business competition to bargaining and public policy.
             Prerequisite: ECON 230
             ECON 410 Capstone Microeconomics                                                        3 cr
             Special Topics in microeconomics. May be repeated up to 3 times. Intended for students pursuing
             Polaris project.
             Prerequisite: ECON 230
             ECON 411 Capstone Macroeconomics                                                        3 cr
             Special Topics in macroeconomics. May be repeated up to 3 times. Intended for students pursuing
             Polaris project.
             Prerequisite: ECON 230
             ECON 412 Capstone in Quantitative Economics                                             3 cr
             Special Topics in Quantitative Economics. May be repeated up to 3 times. Intended for students
             pursuing Polaris project.
             Prerequisite: ECON 230
             ECON 420 Advanced Probability and Statistics                                           3 cr
             Probability and Statistics covers the essential quantitative tools Economists use to analyze
             uncertainty, interpret data, and test theories. Students learn core concepts like probability
             distributions, expected value, and statistical inference, building the foundation needed for empirical
             work in Economics. The course will include theory and applications.
             Prerequisite: ECON 220
                                                                                                     105
             THE UNIVERSITY OF AUSTIN                                      ACADEMIC CATALOG : 2026-2027


########## p-106.jpg ##########
             ECON 421 Causality                                                                      3 cr
             This course introduces the modern toolkit for estimating causal effects from observational and
             experimental data. Students will learn the potential outcomes framework and the main identification
             strategies used in empirical research today, such as difference-in-differences, synthetic control
             methods, regression discontinuity design, and matching.
             Prerequisite: ECON 230
             ECON 422 Time Series Econometrics                                                       3 cr
             This course applies time series techniques to study variation among variables over time.
             Prerequisite: ECON 230
             ECON 423 Econometrics of Panel Data                                                     3 cr
             This course applies advanced Econometric analyses to Economic problems that arise in a panel
             context, combining variation over time with cross-sectional variation at a moment in time.
             Prerequisite: ECON 230
             ECON 430 Advanced Mathematical Economics                                                3 cr
             This course covers micro-founded models of decision making by Economic agents (primarily
             consumers, workers, and firms) rooted in the concept of constrained optimization. We dive deeper
             into some of the canonical models discussed in ECON 201-204, in particular focusing on alternative
             models with different kinds of decision-theoretic foundations that imply different causal outcomes.
             The course concludes with a brief discussion thinking about how such models can be disciplined with
             data. Policy implications, as they pertain to taxation, subsidization, Social Security, and education
             will be considered.
             Prerequisite: ECON 230
                                                                                                      106
             THE UNIVERSITY OF AUSTIN                                      ACADEMIC CATALOG • 2026-2027


########## p-107.jpg ##########
             ECON 431 Advanced Quantitative Economics                                                3cr
             Only so many economic models can actually be analyzed by hand. Instead, more complex models,
             such as those that allow consumers to exhibit differences regarding how they behave, how much
             money they make, etc., require computational tools in order to analyze and understand their
             implications. In this course we use advanced tools from machine learning, artificial intelligence (large
             language models), and neural networks to study the properties of Economic models that feature
             everything from heterogeneous agents to rationality frictions. Computational tools will be used, as
             much of the course will involve coding in Python, R, and Julia.
             Prerequisite: ECON 230
             ECON 435 Applied Dynamic Programming                                                    3cr
             This course introduces the mathematical structure of dynamic programming. This tool is used to study
             dynamic choices by households, firms and dynamic games.
             Prerequisite: ECON 230
             ECON 440 Industrial Organization and Competition Policy                                 3 cr
             Industrial Organization and Competition Policy studies how firms behave in markets with limited
             competition-examining pricing strategies, market power, and the incentives firms face in oligopolies
             and monopolies. Students learn how antitrust laws and regulatory policy attempt to promote
             competition and protect consumers from anticompetitive practices. Offered subject to sufficient
             student interest
             Prerequisite: ECON 230
