########## p-110.jpg ##########
             HIST
             HIST 110  Ancient Greece                                                                3 cr
             This course immerses students in the history and thought of the Classical Greek world. By engaging
             with the most influential ancient Greek historians, dramatists, and philosophers, students gain an
             appreciation for one of the ancient foundations of modern civilization. Readings include Aeschylus'
             Persians, portions of the histories of Herodotus and Thucydides, passages from the Presocratic
             philosophers, Sophocles' Oedipus the King and Antigone, and Plato's Protagoras and Apology. This
             survey of Hellenic culture and literature, which includes attention to historical context and the
             formative reception of these texts and authors, prepares students to recognize the contributions made
             by ancient Greece and Athens in particular to the Western tradition and human culture writ large.
             HIST 111  Ideological Experiments of the 20th Century                                   3 cr
             This course examines the historical context, intellectual origins, and practical consequences of the
             most influential ideological alternatives to liberalism in the twentieth century, with particular
             attention to Soviet Russia, Nazi Germany, Fascist Italy, and Communist China. What economic,
             social, and cultural conditions made communism, Nazism, and fascism seem appealing? How did
             these radical political innovations play out in practice? Students read influential primary sources such
             as Marx and Engels' Communist Manifesto, Gentile and Mussolini's Doctrine of Fascism, and Mao's
             "Sixteen Points" alongside narrative accounts of historical events such as the Russian Revolution, the
             Nazi takeover of Germany, and the Chinese Cultural Revolution.
             HIST 115 Ancient Rome                                                                   3cr
             This course is a general introduction to the cultural and intellectual history of ancient Rome from its
             founding to the high Empire. Why was ancient Rome able to conquer and rule most of the
             Mediterranean world? What did ancient Romans believe? What kind of debates did they have? What
             is distinctive about ancient Roman culture, and how did it change over time? Through independent
             reading, guided reading, lectures, and in-class discussion of Livy, Cicero, Virgil, Seneca, Plutarch, and
             other canonical Roman authors, students in this course acquire a substantial introductory knowledge
             of the cultural, intellectual, and political history of ancient Rome. Students also examine pivotal
             events such as the expulsion of the Tarquins, the Conflict of the Orders, the Punic Wars, and the
             Roman Civil Wars.
                                                                                                     110
             THE UNIVERSITY OF AUSTIN                                      ACADEMIC CATALOG. 2026-2027


########## p-111.jpg ##########
             HIST 130  Rise of the West                                                              3 cr
             This course is an introduction to late antiquity, medieval Europe, and the intellectual traditions that
             shaped the West between the fall of the Roman Empire and the dawn of the Renaissance. How did
             European civilization emerge from the collapse of antiquity? How did the legacy of Greek and Roman
             antiquity, the feudal system, Celtic paganism, Scandinavian heathenism, Christianity, and the
             encounter with Islam shape British and European culture? Students acquire a substantial introductory
             knowledge of the history of medieval Britain and Europe, as well as their relation to the Near East.
             Readings include selections from St. Augustine's Confessions and the Summa Theologica of St.
             Thomas Aquinas, as well as other works of literature, philosophy, and theology that defined the late
             antique and medieval imagination.
             HIST 131 Making of the Modern World                                                     3 cr
             This course examines the beginning of the modern West in the theological controversies, sectarian
             conflict, and philosophical turmoil of early modern Europe and Britain. What was the Reformation,
             and what were its consequences? What was gained and what was lost in the transition from the
             medieval to the modern world? Through careful reading, class discussion, and supplemental lectures,
             students explore new conceptions of religion, politics, individual freedom, and human nature that
             emerged in the fifteenth, sixteenth, and seventeenth centuries. Readings include Machiavelli's Prince,
             Marlowe's Doctor Faustus, and Shakespeare's Hamlet, as well as selections from Luther, Calvin,
             Hobbes, and Locke. As context for the rise of early modern liberalism, including new practices of
             religious toleration, students also examine pivotal events including the Thirty Years' War, the Peace of
             Westphalia, and the British Civil Wars.
             HIST 210 The Enlightenment                                                              3cr
             This course is an introduction to the Enlightenment, with particular attention to its influence on the
             French Revolution. In the wake of the Scientific Revolution, radical thinkers such as Voltaire, Diderot,
             and Kant challenged the traditional authority of the church and crown and advanced new arguments
             for individual liberty. Moral philosophers such as Mandeville and Adam Smith began to suggest that
             self-interest might be harnessed to the public good. Enlightenment ideas helped inspire the American
             Revolution but played out more disastrously in France, where they were mediated by the influence of
             Rousseau. Alongside works of eighteenth-century French and English literature, philosophy, art, and
             music, students learn about the Ancien Régime and reflect on the events of the French Revolution,
             including the storming of the Bastille, the Reign of Terror, the Counter-Revolution in the Vendée, and
             the rise of Napoleon.
                                                                                                     111
             THE UNIVERSITY OF AUSTIN                                      ACADEMIC CATALOG . 2026-2027


########## p-112.jpg ##########
             HIST 211 Renaissance and Reformation                                                    3cr
             This course is a general introduction to the cultural and intellectual history of England and Europe in
             the fifteenth, sixteenth, and seventeenth centuries. How did the feuding city-states of early modern
             Italy produce such extraordinary innovation in the arts? Students read works by notable Renaissance
             humanists such as Petrarch, Erasmus, and Montaigne, trace the revival of Hellenistic schools of
             thought such as Neoplatonism and Skepticism, study artistic movements such as Mannerism and the
             Baroque, and explore the origins of the Reformation.
             Prerequisite: HIST 130 or ALT 1030
             HIST 215  Romanticism and Realism                                                       3 cr
             Why did German and English authors turn against the influence of the French Enlightenment? Did
             the reforms instituted by Napoleon fulfill the aims of the French Revolution? What did Tocqueville
             and Marx think of the "Springtime of Nations"? What was the Paris Commune? This course is a
             general introduction to the restless, rapidly developing cultural, intellectual, and political landscape of
             England, Europe, and Russia in the nineteenth century, including the German Counter-
             Enlightenment, philosophical idealism, the rise and fall of Napoleon Bonaparte, the populist
             Revolutions of 1848, and new experiments in literary and artistic naturalism.
             Prerequisite: HIST 210 or ALT 1040
             HIST 220 Decadence, Modernism, and the Great War                                        3 cr
             How are twentieth-century political movements such as anarchism, Marxism, fascism, and Futurism
             related to each other? What prompted early twentieth-century experiments in art and architecture
             such as Dada, Surrealism, Cubism, Art Nouveau, and Art Deco? How did the film industry begin? This
             course is a general introduction to the turbulent intellectual, cultural, and political landscape of
             England, Europe, and Russia at the end of the nineteenth century and the beginning of the twentieth
             century. What led to the end of the Belle Époque? Students investigate how unprecedented prosperity,
             hopes for a lasting peace, and fin-de-siècle decadence gave way to the "Great War" we now know as
             World War I, as well as the Russian Revolution and the Spanish Civil War.
             Prerequisite: HIST 215 or INF 2200
             HIST 280  Special Topic in History                                                     1.5cr
             This course offers intensive study of a single historical topic or text selected by the instructor. Content
             varies by offering.

             THE UNIVERSITY OF AUSTIN                                      ACADEMIC CATALOG• 2026-2027

             of a faculty member, with content and requirements to be determined in consultation between

             This course offers intensive study of a single historical topic or text selected by the instructor. Content

             This course provides for individualized study of a historical topic, text, or figure under the supervision

             Arts required.
             HIST 310 The Hebrew Bible                                                               3 cr
             How did the Hebrew Bible shape the moral, literary, and religious imagination of the Western world?
             This course introduces students to the books of the Hebrew Bible in their ancient Near Eastern and
             Mediterranean contexts while exploring their literary artistry, theological vision, and enduring
             cultural influence. Students will examine major genres, themes, and interpretive traditions alongside
             the history, archaeology, and societies from which these texts emerged, gaining a deeper appreciation
             for one of history's most influential literary and religious corpora.
             Prerequisite: LITR 103 or INF 1100
                                                                                                     113
             THE UNIVERSITY OF AUSTIN                                      ACADEMIC CATALOG . 2026-2027


########## p-114.jpg ##########
             HIST 315 Classical Historiography                                                       3 cr
             This course explores the classical foundations of Western historical writing. What makes a historical
             account trustworthy, and who decides which stories endure? How should history explain human
             action, political change, and the rise and fall of civilizations? Students read major selections from
             Herodotus, Thucydides, Xenophon, Polybius, Sallust, Livy, and Tacitus and trace the evolution of
             historical explanation. By examining competing models of causation, motivation, political analysis,
             and biography, students learn the craft of history itself.
             Prerequisite: HIST 115 or ALT 1010
             HIST 320 The New Testament                                                              3 cr
             Who was Jesus? Who was the Apostle Paul? How did Christianity emerge within Judaism, then spread
             throughout the entire Roman world and far beyond? This course introduces students to the New
             Testament in the context of ancient Judaism and the early Roman Empire, including questions of
             authorship, genre, theology, ethics, and politics, as well as reception and later influence. Students
             spend most of their time on the practical work of interpretation, asking the big questions that the New
             Testament inspires and evaluating competing claims about its meaning and significance.
             Prerequisite: LITR 103 or INF 1300
             HIST 325 The Roman Republic                                                             3 cr
             How did a small city on the Tiber become the dominant power of the Mediterranean? This course
             explores the history, politics, and culture of the Roman Republic from its legendary beginnings to its
             dramatic collapse. Through the writings of historians, statesmen, generals, and philosophers, students
             examine the development of republican government, the Punic Wars, social conflict, imperial
             expansion, and the rise of Julius Caesar. Alongside literary sources, the course draws upon
             archaeology, art, and material culture to illuminate one of history's most influential political
             experiments. Major authors include Polybius, Dionysius of Halicarnassus, Cato the Elder, Sallust,
             Julius Caesar, Cicero, Livy, Plutarch, Appian, and Cassius Dio. Students may also encounter Cornelius
             Nepos, Valerius Maximus, Aulus Gellius, Strabo, Pliny the Elder, and Velleius Paterculus.
             Prerequisite: HIST 115 or ALT 1010
                                                                                                      114
             THE UNIVERSITY OF AUSTIN                                      ACADEMIC CATALOG : 2026-2027


########## p-115.jpg ##########
             HIST 330 The Roman Empire                                                               3 cr
             How did Rome become the greatest empire in the ancient world, and why did it endure for so long?
             From the rule of Augustus Caesar beginning in 27 BCE to the fall of the Western Roman Empire in 476
             CE, this course examines the political, cultural, intellectual, and religious transformations of imperial
             Rome. Students will study the major emperors, the expansion and administration of the empire, the
             rise of Christianity, monumental architecture, and the flourishing of late antique literature and
             philosophy while engaging enduring debates about the nature of empire and the causes of Rome's
             transformation. Students will study the Second Sophistic, read later Roman authors such as
             Ammianus Marcellinus, Orosius, and Augustine, and grapple with questions posed by Gibbon's great
             work, The History of the Decline and Fall of the Roman Empire.
             Prerequisite: HIST 325
             HIST 335  Plutarch                                                                      3 cr
             What is a hero? What is the use of thinking in terms of the collective self and the collective other in
             history? What does it mean to live ethically within a fractured, morally complex empire? This course
             centers on Plutarch, one of the most influential psychologists and essayists of antiquity. His writings
             offer a toolkit for self-examination, uniting moral insights, biography, and leadership studies, and
             shaped generations of Western statesmen, including the American Founding Fathers. Through close
             readings of Plutarch's Parallel Lives and Moralia, students encounter the tension between high ideals
             and political realities, the psychology of character formation, the nature of lived virtue, and the
             transformative role of mentorship (paideia).
             Prerequisite: HIST 330 or ALT 1010
             HIST 340 Late Antiquity                                                                 3 cr
             Was the fall of Rome a cataclysmic end or a vibrant new beginning? For decades, the centuries
             between the high Roman Empire and the Middle Ages were dismissed as "the Dark Ages." This course
             dismantles that myth, revealing a vibrant era of cultural synthesis, scientific adaptation, and the
             foundation of Christian institutions, alongside the challenge posed by the rise of Islam. Guided by the
             landmark insights of scholars such as Peter Brown, students explore how Greek philosophy, Roman
             administrative structures, Judaism, and Christianity fused to create theological, literary, political, and
             legal frameworks that still govern our world today. This course offers students an immersive
             masterclass in cultural transition, conflict, globalization, and macro-historical analysis, combining the
             serious study of history with an emphasis on primary texts.
             Prerequisite: HIST 115 or ALT 1010
                                                                                                      115
             THE UNIVERSITY OF AUSTIN                                      ACADEMIC CATALOG . 2026-2027


########## p-116.jpg ##########
             HIST345 Early Christian Thought                                                         3 cr
             What are the origins of modern Western ideas about sexuality, bodily autonomy, and identity? This
             course traces the radical intellectual revolutions of early Christianity, exploring how counter-cultural
             ideas of asceticism, monasticism, and martyrdom fundamentally reshaped the Greco-Roman world
             toward what the West would later become. Students dive into fierce theological debates that redefined
             what it meant to be human, parsing the intersections of spirit, society, and authority. Topics include
             the development of Church services, the basics of Christian theology and heresy, and why there are so
             many different kinds of Churches and Christians. Students read a wide array of early Christian
             thinkers in context, including figures such as Irenaeus, Justin Martyr, Tertullian, Clement, Origen,
             Eusebius, Lactantius, Athanasius, John Chrysostom, Ambrose, Ephrem, Augustine, and Justinian,
             among others.
             Prerequisite: LITR 103 or INF 1300
             HIST 350 Judaism in Antiquity                                                           3 cr
             This course examines ancient Judaism as a central intellectual tradition of the classical world and a
             foundation of Western civilization. Through sustained engagement with biblical and pseudepigraphic
             works, Second Temple literature, Rabbinic writings, aspects of Hellenistic-Jewish philosophy, and the
             carliest Christian works read in their Jewish context, students explore the interpretive practices,
             theological ideas, and normative constructs that shaped both Judaism and Christianity. Situating
             these works within the wider culture of antiquity, this course illuminates intellectual and cultural
             crucibles that forged central concepts of the Western tradition, highlighting the integral part played
             by the Jewish tradition in the shaping of the modern world.
             Prerequisite: LITR 103 or INF 1100
             HIST 355  Modern Judaism                                                                3 cr
             This course examines the major movements, debates, and crises that have shaped modern Judaism
             from the eighteenth century to our present time. Beginning with the Jewish Enlightenment and
             Hasidism, it traces the emergence of Jewish responses to emancipation, secularization, nationalism,
             philosophy, religious reform, and modern politics. Students explore the development of modern
             religious philosophy and analyze how major Jewish thinkers engaged with concepts such as reason,
             tradition, ethics, revelation, and collective identity. The course concludes with the theological and
             intellectual aftermath of the Holocaust and the creation of the State of Israel. Through sustained
             engagement with primary texts, students explore the various ways modern Jews have sought to
             reinterpret an ancient tradition under the pressures of modernity.
             Prerequisite: HIST 350
                                                                                                      116
             THE UNIVERSITY OF AUSTIN                                      ACADEMIC CATALOG . 2026-2027


########## p-117.jpg ##########
             HIST 360 Shakespeare: English History Plays I                                           3 cr
             Students read and discuss Shakespeare's plays about the Wars of the Roses in fifteenth-century
             England, including 1-3 Henry VI and Richard III, his later play King John about the struggle for the
             crown in thirteenth-century England, and selections from his historical sources. Why did civil war
             break out? What is the relation between church and state? Students explore Shakespeare's
             understanding of politics, divine providence, historical change, social class, popular protest, and
             effective leadership through memorable characters such as the misguided pacifist Henry VI, his
             tempestuous wife Margaret of Anjou, the comic demagogue Jack Cade, and the ruthless Machiavel
             Richard III.
             Prerequisite: HIST 130 or ALT 1020
             HIST 361  Shakespeare: English History Plays II                                         3cr
             Students read and discuss Shakespeare's plays about the end of the Plantagenet dynasty and the rise of
             the House of Lancaster in fifteenth-century England, Richard II, 1-2 Henry IV, and Henry V, a
             sequence also known as his "Henriad," as well as his later play Henry VIII and selections from his
             historical sources. What makes one king better than another? What is the cost of stable, competent,
             and fair-minded leadership? Students explore Shakespeare's understanding of politics, divine
             providence, historical change, social class, crime, statesmanship, international law, and the ethics of
             war through memorable characters such as the flamboyant narcissist Richard II, the bombastic
             swindler John Falstaff, the beleaguered innkeeper Mistress Quickly, the hotheaded northern rebel
             Harry "Hotspur" Percy, and the heroic, introspective, morally disconcerting Henry V.
             Prerequisite: HIST 360
             HIST 365 The French Revolution                                                          3 cr
             The French Revolution was a defining moment in modern history, setting important precedents for
             political violence, radical democracy, totalitarianism, and the restructuring of social hierarchy. This
             course examines the Revolution as a case-study in competing explanations of historical change,
             including the collapse of the Ancien Régime, the Reign of Terror, the counter-revolution in the
             Vendée, and the rise of Napoleon. Why did the French Revolution play out differently from the
             American? Students consider responses by contemporary critics such as Burke and de Maistre, as well
             as the reception of the Revolution by competing schools of modern historiography: as inevitability, as
             symbol, as tragedy, and as cautionary tale.
             Prerequisite: HIST 210 or INF 2121
                                                                                                     117
             THE UNIVERSITY OF AUSTIN                                      ACADEMIC CATALOG. 2026-2027


########## p-118.jpg ##########
             HIST 370 Historiography and the Philosophy of History                                   3 cr
             This course examines classical, medieval, modern, and postmodern conceptions of history. How did a
             sense of history emerge in the West, and how did it change over time? How does a civilization's sense
             of history shape its social and political outlook? How did history become an object of scientific study?
             Can history provide us with knowledge of man and his destiny, or has historicism left us nihilistic and
             rudderless? Students read classic authors representative of turning points in Western historical
             thinking, ranging from classical historians such as Polybius, Sallust, and Tacitus to more modern
             figures such as Condorcet, Hegel, von Ranke, Marx, and Nietzsche.
             Prerequisite: HIST 115 or ALT 1010
             HIST 375 The Changing Structure of Civilization                                         3 cr
             This course examines nineteenth- and twentieth-century theories of civilization that reject the
             modernization thesis: the view that history advances through a rational process of development
             culminating in secular, liberal, post-ethnonational societies. The thinkers studied here instead
             understand civilizations as historically particular forms shaped by ethnos, religion, tradition, class
             and elite formation, and cultural "style." Some are profoundly pessimistic, diagnosing inevitable
             cycles of rise and decline; others seek the renewal of the West through the establishing of "alternative"
             modernities. By situating these thinkers within a continuous tradition of Counter-Enlightenment
             thought, the course asks whether modernity represents progress or exhaustion and whether
             Enlightenment universalism marks the culmination of European civilization or its dissolution.
             Students learn to historicize twentieth-century radical and revolutionary movements, reconstruct the
             civilizational diagnoses from which they emerged, and explain their appeal to contemporaries.
             Prerequisite: HIST 210 or INF 2121
             HIST 379 Special Topic in History                                                      1.5 crl
             This course offers intensive study of a single historical topic or text selected by the instructor. Content
             varies by offering.

             THE UNIVERSITY OF AUSTIN                                      ACADEMIC CATALOG : 2026-2027


########## p-119.jpg ##########
             HIST 380 Postmodernism and Postcolonialism                                              3 cr
             Is history meaningful? Does it have a direction or a purpose? This course introduces the concept of
             postmodernism and explores its intellectual origins and influence, within the larger context of the
             collapse of Communism at the end of the twentieth century and the current crisis of liberalism. By way
             of comparison, this course also introduces the intellectual and cultural history of European
             colonialism, decolonization, and postcolonialism. What is the intellectual and political legacy of
             European imperialism?
             Prerequisite: HIST 111 or INF 2300
             HIST 381  Independent Study in History                                                 1.5cr

             LANG
             LANG 310 Ancient Greek |                                                                3 cr
             This course initiates students into a knowledge of ancient Greek, focusing on the grammar, syntax,
             and vocabulary of standard Classical Attic Greek. Students also come into contact with Homeric,
             Ionic, Hellenistic, and Koine Greek, and emerge with a basic understanding of and reading facility in
             Classical Greek sufficient to approach basic reading and further study. This course is the first part of a
             year-long introductory curriculum.
             LANG 311 Ancient Greek II                                                               3 cr
             This course continues the introduction to the basics of ancient Greek. This course is the second part of
             a year-long introductory curriculum.
             Prerequisite: LANG 310
             LANG 312 Ancient Greek III                                                              3 cr
             This course concludes the introduction to the basics of ancient Greek. This course is the third and final
             part of a year-long introductory curriculum.
             Prerequisite: LANG 311
             LANG 319 Directed Readings in Ancient Greek                                             3 cr
             This course builds upon skills learned in Ancient Greek I, II, and III and immerses students in ancient
             Greek literature, whether prose or poetry. This course assumes a basic command of Greek grammar
             and syntax and a vocabulary sufficient to sustain continued linguistic development.
             Prerequisite: LANG 312

             THE UNIVERSITY OF AUSTIN                                      ACADEMIC CATALOG  .2026-2027

########## p-127.jpg ##########
             LITR
             LITR 102  Epic and Tragedy                                                              3 cr
             This course covers three foundational texts of Greek literature: Homer's Odyssey, Aeschylus' Oresteia,
             and Euripides' Bacchae. Through close reading, class discussion, and some consideration of historical
             context, students learn to recognize and understand the significance of characters such as Odysseus,
             Clytemnestra, and Dionysus as they appear and re-appear over a range of times and places. Students
             become conversant in many narrative and artistic reference points of Greek mythology and consider
             how each text presents the relationships between individual, community, and cosmos.
             LITR 103 The Bible                                                                      3 cr
             This course orients students to the history, literature, interpretation, and reception of the Bible,
             including the Hebrew Scriptures and the New Testament. Students will encounter the wide range of
             literary genres within the Bible, come to appreciate the historical-and text-critical issues connected to
             the scriptural writings, and gain an understanding of how biblical texts have shaped distinct cultures
             and world history. Beginning with the legal, prophetic, historical, and poetic texts of the Hebrew
             Bible and ending with the Gospels, Acts, epistles, and apocalypse of the New Testament, this class
             prepares students to navigate a world and apprehend a culture that have been fundamentally shaped
             by the Jewish and Christian Bibles and within which those Bibles remain salient touchpoints for
             understanding how many people think and act.
             LITR 210  Medieval Literature                                                           3 cr
             This course introduces students to acclaimed and influential works of literature written in the Middle
             Ages. Students encounter genres such as epic, romance, lyric, allegory, and dream vision while
             exploring the worlds of chivalry, courtly love, pilgrimage, and Christian devotion. Authors may
             include Chaucer, Marie de France, the Pearl Poet, Dante, and Malory, as well as selections from Old
             English and Norse literature.
             LITR 215  Shakespeare                                                                   3 cr
             Students read and discuss Shakespeare's most important tragedies and romances, including Macbeth,
             Othello, and King Lear, as well as Cymbeline, The Winter's Tale, and The Tempest. Through close
             reading and discussion, students consider Shakespeare's treatment of ambition, justice, repentance,
             forgiveness, political authority, family, and redemption. Attention is also given to Shakespeare's
             language and lasting influence on English literature.
                                                                                                     127
             THE UNIVERSITY OF AUSTIN                                      ACADEMIC CATALOG . 2026-2027


########## p-128.jpg ##########
             LITR 220 Milton                                                                         3cr
             Students read and discuss Milton's Paradise Lost, Paradise Regained, and Samson Agonistes.
             Alongside these major works, students read Milton's shorter poetry and prose while considering his
             historical context, political thought, theology, classical learning, and extraordinary influence on later
             literature. The course emphasizes close reading of Milton's language as well as the enduring questions
             his poetry raises about freedom, authority, and the justice of God.
             Prerequisite: HIST 130 or INF 2121
             LITR 225  Eighteenth-Cent ury Literature                                                3 cr
             This course introduces students to acclaimed and influential works of literature written in the Long
             Eighteenth Century, from the Restoration through the Regency era, including the rise of
             Romanticism. Students explore the emergence of satire, the novel, lyric poetry, and literary
             criticism alongside debates about reason, sentiment, imagination, and political liberty. Authors may
             include Dryden, Swift, Pope, Johnson, Burney, Blake, Wordsworth, Coleridge, Austen, and Keats,
             among others.
             Prerequisite: HIST 210 or INF 2121
             LITR 230 Literary Criticism                                                             3 cr
             What is the skill of reading well? How should we read a poem, a novel, or a play? This course
             introduces students to major works of literary criticism from the twentieth century. Students read
             influential critics and schools of criticism while developing practical skills in literary interpretation,
             analysis, and argument. Emphasis is placed on applying competing critical methods to literary texts
             and evaluating the strengths and limitations of different approaches to interpretation.
             LITR 280 Special Topic in Literature                                                   1.5cr
             This course offers intensive study of a single literary topic or text selected by the instructor. Content
             varies by offering.
             LITR 281 Independent Study in Literature                                               1.5 cr
             This course provides for individualized study of a literary area, topic, text, or figure under the
             supervision of a faculty member, with content and requirements to be determined in consultation
             between the student and instructor. Permission of instructor, Academic Concentration Lead, and
             Dean of Arts required.

             LITR 310 The Trojan War                                                                 3 cr
             Why has the Trojan War captivated readers for nearly three thousand years? This course explores the
             myths, heroes, and moral dilemmas associated with this legendary conflict, beginning with Homer's
             Iliad and continuing into related classical Greek tragedies: Sophocles' Ajax and Philoctetes and
             Euripides' Hecuba, Trojan Women, and Iphigenia at Aulis. What is the human cost of ambition?
             Students explore the nature of heroism, leadership, justice, war, grief, anger, and revenge, as well as
             the relation between the divine and the human.
             LITR 315  Hesiod and Ovid                                                               3 cr
             Why did the Greeks and Romans tell stories about gods, monsters, and heroes? Through readings
             from Hesiod's Theogony and Ovid's Metamorphoses, as well as representative responses from the
             medieval or early modern period, students explore the creation of the cosmos, the Olympian gods,
             heroic legend, and the myths that Western authors long assumed every educated reader knew. Along
             the way, students discover how these stories informed ancient society and continue to inspire
             literature, art, and philosophy. Students may also read the Homeric Hymns.
                                                                                                     129
             THE UNIVERSITY OF AUSTIN                                      ACADEMIC CATALOG . 2026-2027


########## p-130.jpg ##########
             LITR 320 Comedy                                                                         3cr
             What is comedy? How does it work? How has it changed over time? This course traces the history of
             comedy from antiquity to the present day. Students read plays by authors such as Aristophanes,
             Plautus, Molière, Gay, Wilde, and Stoppard, as well as selections from secondary sources such as
             Hutcheson, Thoughts on Laughter, and Freud, Jokes and Their Relation to the Unconscious. Students
             may also read short stories and watch films.
             LITR 325  Tragedy                                                                       3cr
             What is tragedy? How does it work? How has it changed over time? This course traces the history of
             tragedy from antiquity to the present day. Students read plays such as Sophocles, Oedipus at Colonus;
             Miller, Death of a Salesman; Beckett, Waiting for Godot; and Soyinka, Death and the King's
             Horseman, as well as selections from secondary sources such as Steiner, The Death of Tragedy, and
             Nuttall, Why Does Tragedy Give Pleasure? Students may also read short stories and watch films.
             LITR 330 Lyric Poetry                                                                   3cr
             What is the lyric? How does it work? How has it changed over time? This course traces the history of
             lyric poetry from antiquity to the present day. Students read lyric poems by authors such as Sappho,
             Horace, Petrarch, John Donne, William Wordsworth, and T. S. Eliot, as well as selections from notable
             critics such as Helen Vendler, Jonathan Culler, and others. Students may also listen to music.
             LITR 335 Classical Aesthetics                                                           3 cr
             What is beauty? Why do works of art move us? What makes literature great? This course traces the
             origins of aesthetic and literary criticism in classical antiquity through foundational texts including
             Plato, Ion; Aristotle, Poetics; Longinus, On the Sublime; and Horace, Ars Poetica. Students explore
             ancient theories of beauty, imitation, rhetoric, and the sublime as well as their reception from the
             Renaissance through the Neoclassical aesthetics of the eighteenth century. Early modern sources may
             include Sidney's Apology for Poetry, Corneille's Three Discourses, and Winckelmann's "Thoughts on
             the Imitation of the Greek Works," among others.
             Prerequisite: PHIL 120 or INF 1110
                                                                                                     130
             THE UNIVERSITY OF AUSTIN                                      ACADEMIC CATALOG : 2026-2027


########## p-131.jpg ##########
             LITR 340 Dante, Divine Comedy I                                                         3 cr
             Students read and discuss Dante's Inferno and the first half of his Purgatorio. Students explore Dante's
             historical context, poetic imagination, theological vision, political thought, and extraordinary
             synthesis of classical and Christian traditions. Students also consider the lasting influence of The
             Divine Comedy on literature, philosophy, and the arts.
             Prerequisite: HIST 130 or ALT 1020
             LITR 341  Dante, Divine Comedy II                                                       3 cr
             Students read and discuss the second half of Dante's Purgatorio and his Paradiso, completing their
             study of his Divine Comedy. Students explore Dante's historical context, poetic imagination,
             theological vision, political thought, and extraordinary synthesis of classical and Christian traditions.
             Students also consider the lasting influence of The Divine Comedy on literature, philosophy, and
             the arts.
             Prerequisite: LITR 340
             LITR 345 Shakespeare: Roman Plays                                                       3 cr
             Students read and discuss Shakespeare's works about ancient Rome, including Titus Andronicus,
             Julius Caesar, Coriolanus, Antony and Cleopatra, and his epyllion, The Rape of Lucrece, as well as
             selections from his source material, typically drawn from Plutarch's Lives. The course explores
             Shakespeare's engagement with Roman history, republicanism, imperial power, civic virtue, and
             political ambition, as well as his reception of Roman authors such as Livy, Virgil, Ovid, and Plutarch.
             Prerequisite: HIST 115 or ALT 1010
             LITR 350 Shakespeare: Comedies and Problem Plays                                        3 cr
             Students read and discuss Shakespeare's most important comedies and problem plays, including A
             Midsummer Night's Dream, The Merchant of Venice, Much Ado about Nothing, As You Like It,
             Twelfth Night, and Measure for Measure. Students examine Shakespeare's treatment of love,
             marriage, identity, justice, and social order while exploring the comic imagination in all its variety.
             Students also consider the distinctive challenges posed by the so-called "problem plays" and
             Shakespeare's remarkable experimentation with dramatic form.
                                                                                                     131
             THE UNIVERSITY OF AUSTIN                                      ACADEMIC CATALOG. 2026-2027


########## p-132.jpg ##########
             LITR 355  Early Modern Literature                                                       3 cr
             Why and how did literature flourish in Renaissance Europe? This course introduces students to
             acclaimed and influential works of literature written in the sixteenth and seventeenth centuries.
             Students read major works of poetry, drama, and prose while examining the rise of the theater and the
             influence of early modern skepticism, religious conflict, and political revolution. Authors may include
             Sidney, Spenser, Shakespeare, Donne, Herbert, and Marvell, among others.
             Prerequisite: HIST 130 or INF 2121
             LITR 360 Victorian Literature                                                           3 cr
             This course introduces students to acclaimed and influential works of literature written in the
             nineteenth century. Students examine how novelists, poets, and essayists responded to
             industrialization, empire, religious doubt, scientific discovery, and rapid social change. Authors
             may include Dickens, the Brontës, Tennyson, Browning, Arnold, Hopkins, Wilde, and Hardy,
             among others.
             Prerequisite: PHIL 130 or INF 2121
             LITR 365 Modern Literature                                                              3 cr
             What does it mean to be modern? This course introduces students to acclaimed and influential works
             of literature written in the twentieth century. Students explore the literary innovations of modernism
             and its aftermath while considering themes such as war, memory, identity, alienation, and the search
             for meaning in the modern world. Authors may include Yeats, Eliot, Woolf, Joyce, Faulkner, and
             Beckett, among others.
             Prerequisite: PHIL 130 or INF 2121
             LITR 380 Special Topic in Literature                                                   1.5cr
             This course offers intensive study of a single literary topic or text selected by the instructor. Content
             varies by offering.
             LITR 381 Independent Study in Literature                                               1.5 cr
             This course provides for individualized study of a literary area, topic, text, or figure under the
             supervision of a faculty member, with content and requirements to be determined in consultation
             between the student and instructor. Permission of instructor, Academic Concentration Lead, and
             Dean of Arts required.

             This seminar offers intensive study of major works of literary nonfiction selected by the instructor,

             LITR 430 The Theory Wars                                                                3 cr
             This course introduces students to twentieth-century controversies about methods of interpretation in
             the humanities. How should we interpret a cultural artifact such as painting or a poem? Students
             consider the relative merits of formalism, historicism, and psychoanalysis, as well as arguments
            for and against "critique" in light of movements such as New Criticism, Deconstruction, and
             New Historicism.

             MATH
             MATH 101 Single-Variable Calculus                                                       3 cr
             Intuitive/practical overview of differential and integral calculus, with applications relevant to science
             and engineering. This course fulfills the calculus requirement for Sciences concentrations, as normally
             expected of incoming freshmen, and is open to students seeking a Sciences concentration who need a
             freshman course to meet that expectation.
             MATH 200 Mathematical Reasoning & Proof                                                 3 cr
             Propositional and predicate logic; sets, functions, and relations; proof by induction, contradiction,
             and construction; cardinality and the infinite; first algebraic structures.
             MATH 210 Linear Algebra                                                                 3 cr
             Intuitive/practical overview of linear algebra, using matrices and finite-dimensional linear transforms.
             Vector and matrix basics, decomposition of matrices by Gauss-Jordan elimination, four subspaces,
             projections, eigenvalues/vectors, PSD matrices, SVD. Applications in science and engineering.
             MATH 220   Probability                                                                  3 cr
             Practical survey of basic probability for Sciences. Continuous and discrete random variables,
             combinatorics and counting, info entropy, Bayes theorem.
             Prerequisite: MATH 101

             PHIL
             PHIL 120  Plato and Aristotle                                                           3cr
             This course allows students to begin to explore Plato's thought in more depth and introduces them to
             the moral and political philosophy of his most important student, Aristotle. How can we know what is
             true? Does virtue lead to happiness? What is the relation between ethics and politics? Students begin
             by reading Plato's best-known and most influential dialogue, the Republic, then turn to selections
             from Aristotle's Nicomachean Ethics and Politics. What is the greatest good? Students consider how
             Aristotle responds to Plato, with particular attention to points of departure. Is virtue a form of
             knowledge? Why do people sometimes lack self-control? What does each philosopher consider the
             best kind of political regime?
                                                                                                     141
             THE UNIVERSITY OF AUSTIN                                      ACADEMIC CATALOG • 2026-2027


########## p-142.jpg ##########
             PHIL 130 The Scientific Revolution                                                      3cr
             This course introduces students to the philosophical innovations that we now describe as the
             Scientific Revolution. What do we mean when we talk about "the scientific method"? And what is its
             proper scope? Students learn how nominalism, mechanism, Baconian empiricism, and Cartesian
             rationalism emerged in the seventeenth century as challengers to Judaism, Christianity, and the
             influence of Aristotle, responding to the pressure introduced by the Reformation, the European wars
             of religion, and the Renaissance revival of Hellenistic skepticism. Do immaterial entities such as God,
             souls, essences, or Plato's Forms exist? Does anything have an intrinsic purpose? Students register
             early successes of the scientific method such as Galileo's discoveries, the Royal Society, Newtonian
             physics, and the Industrial Revolution and grapple with important arguments against its over-
             extension such as Swift's Gulliver's Travels and Hayek's Counter-Revolution.
             PHIL 210 Hellenistic and Roman Philosophy                                               3cr
             How should we live? What is happiness? Can reason overcome suffering? This course explores the
             great philosophical schools that inherited and transformed the legacies of Plato and Aristotle in the
             Hellenistic and Roman worlds. Through close readings of Epicurean, Stoic, Skeptical, Cynic, and
             Neoplatonic authors, students examine competing answers to questions about knowledge, emotion,
             freedom, and virtue. Readings include selections from Hellenistic and Roman thinkers such as
             Epicurus, Lucretius, Cicero, Seneca, Epictetus, Marcus Aurelius, Sextus Empiricus, and Plotinus.
             Prerequisite: PHIL 120 or INF 1110
             PHIL 215  Medieval Philosophy                                                           3 cr
             This course surveys the philosophical traditions of the Middle Ages across Jewish, Christian, and
             Islamic thought. Topics include the reception of Aristotle, the relationship between faith and reason,
             the existence and nature of God, and the problem of universals and may extend to others such as free
             will, providence, and natural law. Students engage closely with primary texts from major figures such
             as Augustine, Boethius, Anselm, Averroes, Maimonides, Aquinas, Duns Scotus, and PHIL 120 or INF
             1110 Ockham.
                                                                                                     142
             THE UNIVERSITY OF AUSTIN                                      ACADEMIC CATALOG • 2026-2027


########## p-143.jpg ##########
             PHIL 220 Early Modern Philosophy                                                        3 cr
             This course surveys the philosophical traditions of seventeenth- and eighteenth-century Europe,
             organized around the contrast between continental rationalism and British empiricism. Topics
             include the nature and scope of knowledge, the relationship between mind and body, and substance
             and causation, and may extend to others such as the foundations of science and morality. Students
             engage closely with primary texts from major figures including Descartes, Spinoza, and Leibniz as
             representative rationalists and Locke, Berkeley, and Hume as representative empiricists.
             Prerequisite: PHIL 130 or INF 1110
             PHIL 225  German Idealism                                                               3 cr
             This course examines the philosophical movement that emerged in Germany in the wake of Kant's
             critical philosophy. Topics include the nature and limits of knowledge, the relationship between
             freedom and necessity, and the structure of self-consciousness and may extend to other subjects such
             as the development of systematic accounts of nature, history, and spirit. Students engage closely with
             selections from primary texts by Kant, Fichte, Hegel, and Schelling.
             Prerequisite: PHIL 220 or ALT 4510B
             PHIL 230 Twentieth- Century Philosophy                                                  3 cr
             This course offers students an introductory survey of the two major traditions that shaped twentieth-
             century philosophy: the continental and the analytic. On the continental side, the course begins with
             phenomenology and proceeds through significant movements such as existentialism, deconstruction,
             and post-structuralism, including readings from figures such as Husserl, Heidegger, Sartre, Merleau-
             Ponty, Levinas, Derrida, Deleuze, and Foucault. On the analytic side, it proceeds from foundational
             figures such as Frege, Russell, Moore, and Wittgenstein to later figures such as Quine.
             Prerequisite: PHIL 225
             PHIL 280 Special Topic in Philosophy                                                   1.5cr
             This course offers intensive study of a single philosophical or theological topic or text selected by the
             instructor. Content varies by offering.
             PHIL 281 Independent Study in Philosophy                                               1.5cr
             This course provides for individualized study of a philosophical or theological area, topic, text, or
             figure under the supervision of a faculty member, with content and requirements to be determined in
             consultation between the student and instructor. Permission of instructor, Academic Concentration
             Lead, and Dean of Arts required.

             PHIL 310 Plato's Dialogues I                                                            3 cr
             What is justice? Can virtue be taught? What is the nature of knowledge? Building on students' initial
             encounter with Plato in the Intellectual Foundations sequence, this course explores a selection of
             Plato's introductory and intermediate dialogues through careful reading and discussion, including his
             Meno and Gorgias. Students examine the philosophical arguments, literary artistry, and historical
             context of these works while engaging the questions that established Plato as one of the foundational
             thinkers of the Western tradition.
             PHIL 315  Plato's Dialogues II                                                          3 cr
             How does philosophical inquiry transform the way we understand reality, knowledge, and the soul?
             This seminar continues the study of Plato through close engagement with some of his intermediate
             and advanced dialogues, including his Symposium. Students grapple with Plato's more complicated
             philosophical thought while considering its historical setting and its extraordinary influence on later
             philosophy, theology, and political thought.
             Prerequisite: PHIL 310 or INF 1200
                                                                                                     144
             THE UNIVERSITY OF AUSTIN                                      ACADEMIC CATALOG . 2026-2027


########## p-145.jpg ##########
             PHIL 320 Neoplatonism and Mysticism                                                     3 cr
             This course traces the remarkable transformation of Platonic philosophy in late antiquity and the
             medieval world through the rise of Neoplatonism and its engagement with mystical and ascetic
             traditions. Students read major Neoplatonic philosophers such as Plotinus, Porphyry, Iamblichus, and
             Proclus alongside Jewish, Christian, and Islamic thinkers who adapted Platonic ideas in new ways.
             The course explores themes including beauty, contemplation, mystical union, prayer, and the ascent
             of the soul while introducing students to traditions such as Kabbalah, Hesychasm, and Sufism.
             Prerequisite: HIST 110 or INF 1200
             PHIL 325  Classical Political Philosophy                                                3 cr
             What is the blueprint for a just society? What are the responsibilities of a citizen? This course traces
             the evolution of classical political thought beyond Plato and Aristotle as it adapted to shifting empires
             and global transformations. What happened when Greek theory met Roman reality? How did the
             advent of Christianity affect normative reasoning? Students engage with a dynamic lineage that
             includes Cicero and Augustine, as well as other political thinkers such as Aristophanes, Xenophon,
             Polybius, and Plutarch.
             Prerequisite: HIST 115 or ALT 1010
             PHIL 330 Early Modern Political Philosophy                                              3cr
             This course examines early modern political philosophy. Topics may include the state of nature, the
             grounds and limits of political authority, republicanism, natural rights, property, liberty, and
             sovereignty, among others. Students engage closely with primary texts from major figures such as
             Machiavelli, Hobbes, Locke, and Montesquieu.
             Prerequisite: HIST 130 or INF 2121
             PHIL 335  Rousseau                                                                      3 cr
             This course offers a close study of the philosophy of Jean-Jacques Rousseau, attending to both his
             philosophical and his literary works. Topics may include the state of nature, the origin and effects of
             inequality, the general will, the foundations of legitimate political authority, education and human
             formation, and the relationship between civilization and authenticity, among others. Students engage
             closely with major texts from across Rousseau's oeuvre.
             Prerequisite: HIST 210 or INF 2121
                                                                                                     145
             THE UNIVERSITY OF AUSTIN                                      ACADEMIC CATALOG . 2026-2027


########## p-146.jpg ##########
             PHIL 340  Existentialism                                                                3 cr
             This course surveys the existentialist tradition, beginning with its nineteenth-century precursors and
             proceeding through its twentieth-century development. Topics may include anxiety and despair,
             freedom and responsibility, authenticity, the absurd, meaning, and existence, among others. Students
             engage closely with primary texts from major figures such as Dostoevsky, Kierkegaard, Heidegger,
             Sartre, and Camus.
             PHIL 345  Nietzsche                                                                     3 cr
             This course offers a close study of the philosophy of Friedrich Nietzsche. Topics include the critique of
             morality, the will to power, the death of God, nihilism, the eternal recurrence, and the Übermensch.
             Students engage closely with major texts from Nietzsche's oeuvre, including On the Genealogy of
             Morals and Beyond Good and Evil.
             Prerequisite: PHIL 120 or INF 1200
             PHIL 350 Modern Aesthetics                                                              3 cr
             This course surveys the philosophical study of art and beauty from the eighteenth century to the
             present. Topics include the nature of beauty and aesthetic judgment, the sublime, the value of art, and
             more. Readings include selections from Burke's Philosophical Enquiry, Kant's Critique of Judgment,
             Hegel's Lectures on Aesthetics, and Nietzsche's Birth of Tragedy.
             Prerequisite: PHIL 225 or ALT 4510B
             PHIL 355 Heidegger                                                                      3 cr
             This course offers a close study of the philosophy of Martin Heidegger. Topics may include the
             question of being, Dasein, existence, temporality, technology, language, and the history of
             metaphysics, among others. Students engage closely with major texts from across Heidegger's oeuvre,
            including his 1927 magnum opus, Being and Time, as well as selections from his later work.
             Prerequisite: PHIL 130 or ALT 4510B
                                                                                                      146
             THE UNIVERSITY OF AUSTIN                                      ACADEMIC CATALOG : 2026-2027


########## p-147.jpg ##########
             PHIL 360 Phenomenology                                                                  3cr
             This course surveys the tradition of modern phenomenology from its origins in Husserl through later
             developments. Topics may include intentionality, the phenomenological method and the epoché, the
             structure of consciousness, embodiment, intersubjectivity, and the relationship between self and
             other, among others. Students engage closely with a range of primary texts from major figures such as
             Husserl, Heidegger, Sartre, Merleau-Ponty, and Levinas.
             Prerequisite: PHIL 225 or ALT 4510B
             PHIL 365  Philosophy of Religion                                                        3 cr
             This course surveys central problems in the philosophy of religion. Topics may include arguments for
             and against the existence of God, the problem of evil, the relationship between faith and reason,
             religious experience, miracles, and the diversity of religious traditions, among others.
             Prerequisite: LITR 103 or INF 1300
             PHIL 370 Critical Theory                                                                3 cr
             This course surveys the Frankfurt School and its contemporary reception in the Anglophone
             humanities and social sciences. Topics may include the critique of instrumental reason, ideology and
             false consciousness, the culture industry, and communicative rationality, among others. The course
             concludes with attention to later extensions and political applications of the Frankfurt School such as
             Critical Race Theory. Students engage closely with primary texts from major figures such as
             Horkheimer, Adorno, Marcuse, and Habermas.
             Prerequisite: HIST 210 or INF 2121
             PHIL 380 Special Topic in Philosophy                                                   1.5 cr
             This course offers intensive study of a single philosophical or theological topic or text selected by the
             instructor. Content varies by offering.
             PHIL 381  Independent Study in Philosophy                                              1.5cr
             This course provides for individualized study of a philosophical or theological area, topic, text, or
             figure under the supervision of a faculty member, with content and requirements to be determined in
             consultation between the student and instructor. Permission of instructor, Academic Concentration
             Lead, and Dean of Arts required.

########## p-149.jpg ##########
             PHIL 420 Analytic Philosophy                                                            3cr
             This course offers an in-depth study of the analytic tradition, from its foundational figures through its
             major twentieth-century developments. Areas covered may include philosophy of language,
             philosophy of mind, and philosophy of science, among others. Students will engage closely with
             primary texts from major figures such as Frege, Russell, Moore, Wittgenstein, Carnap, Quine, Austin,
             and Putnam.
             Prerequisite: PHIL 230
             PHIL 425 Applied Ethics                                                                 3 cr
             This course examines the application of ethical theory to practical moral problems within one or more
             domains selected by the instructor. Topics may include bioethics, business ethics, environmental
             ethics, war and violence, or criminal justice, among others. Topics and texts vary by offering.
             Prerequisite: PHIL 120 or INF 1110
             PHIL 430 Uses and Abuses of Technology                                                  3 cr
             This seminar examines the advantages and disadvantages of technology from a broadly human
             perspective. What is technology? How has it shaped human life? What are its intellectual
             presuppositions, social conditions, benefits, and dangers? Readings may be drawn from philosophy,
             theory, empirical non-fiction, and fiction.
             Prerequisite: PHIL 130 or INF 2121
             PHIL 435 Philosophy of Social Science                                                   3 cr
             This course explores the philosophical foundations of the social sciences. How do reason and morality
             shape economic behavior? What are the limits of knowledge and prediction in complex economic
             systems? How do rules, values, and institutions influence economic and political life? Readings may
             be drawn from both philosophy and the social sciences.

########## p-155.jpg ##########
             POLR
             POLR 110  Polaris Launch                                                                3cr
             This course provides a forum for students to workshop their Polaris projects. Students start thinking
             seriously about the problem, opportunity, or question they wish to pursue and work intensively on
             producing and pitching a polished proposal. Along the way, they build foundational skills for carrying
             a project forward and develop the capacity to independently identify and acquire unfamiliar
             knowledge and capabilities. At the conclusion of the course, they will submit a detailed project
             proposal for final approval.
             POLR 120 Startup Theory                                                                 3 cr
             Startup Theory examines influential theories, frameworks, and mental models commonly used to
             understand startups, entrepreneurship, and technological change. Students engage with ideas
             concerning invention, startups, technological change, disruption, incentives, scaling, design,
             computer science, and the general development of new ventures. The course emphasizes the
             arguments behind these frameworks, their assumptions, and their application to real companies and
             entrepreneurial decisions.
             POLR 210 Vibecoding Hardware                                                            3 cr
             Introduces the use of artificial intelligence tools to design, program, and build functional physical
             systems. Students work with microcontrollers, single-board computers, sensors, motors, and other
             electronic components while using AI to generate code, troubleshoot problems, and accelerate
             prototyping. The course emphasizes learning how hardware and software interact, evaluating AI-
             generated work critically, and moving from an idea to a functioning device.
             Prerequisite: POLR 110
             POLR 211 Computing in Practice                                                          3 cr
             Provides students outside computer science with the technical knowledge necessary to understand,
             evaluate, and participate meaningfully in software-based work. Students examine the practical
             foundations of programming, computer systems, the internet, databases, APIs, cloud infrastructure,
             and artificial intelligence through applied exercises and projects. The course emphasizes functional
             technical literacy, applied problem-solving, and the ability to understand, modify, and create
             technological systems.
             Prerequisite: POLR 110
                                                                                                     155
             THE UNIVERSITY OF AUSTIN                                      ACADEMIC CATALOG. 2026-2027


########## p-156.jpg ##########
             POLR 212 Autodidacticism                                                                3 cr
             Students examine and practice autodidacticism as a method of learning by pursuing several areas of
             serious personal interest over the course of the term. For each area, students establish learning
             objectives, identify and use appropriate resources and methods, document and assess their progress,
             and revise their approach in response to difficulty or failure. By working across multiple subjects,
             students compare learning strategies, evaluate their effectiveness, and develop the capacity to
             independently acquire new knowledge and skills.
             Prerequisite: POLR 110
             POLR 310  Polaris Build                                                                1.5 cr
             In this independent study course, students build their projects. Repeatable for credit.
             Prerequisite: POLR 110
             POLR 311 Polaris Build                                                                  3 cr
             In this independent study course, students build their projects. Repeatable for credit.
             Prerequisite: POLR 110
             POLR 312 From Prototype to Production                                                   3 cr
             This course examines how physical products are designed and built at different stages of development,
             from early prototypes through small-batch and production-scale manufacturing. Students study
             components such as actuators, sensors, electronics, and mechanical systems alongside fabrication
             processes including 3D printing, CNC machining, molding, and other manufacturing methods. The
             course emphasizes how materials, components, processes, and design decisions change as a product
             advances in scale, precision, reliability, and manufacturability.
             Prerequisite: POLR 110
             POLR 313  Polaris Skills                                                                3 cr
             Polaris Skills is a practical, high-intensity class designed to equip students with the foundational
             capabilities needed to bring any idea to life. Students learn tangible skills like accounting, recruiting,
             coding, vibecoding, fundraising, pitching, digital design, and various forms of AI. The course develops
             technical fluency, project management capabilities, and the analytical judgment required to advance
             complex projects.
             Prerequisite: POLR 110

             THE UNIVERSITY OF AUSTIN                                      ACADEMIC CATALOG. 2026-2027

             The concluding course of the Polaris curriculum. Students continue working on their Polaris Projects

             development after UATX. At the conclusion of the course, all students deliver a final presentation.
             Students who have not already done so also publicly release their projects.

             SCIM
             SCIM 101  Quantitative Reasoning - Analysis                                             3 cr

             physics, biology, and economics; there will be particular emphasis on the laws of nature and analogies

             course examples will be drawn from a variety of fields including physics, biology, and economics;

             SCIM 110 Introduction to Physics                                                       3 cr

             and magnetism.
             SCIM 210 Life Sciences, Ethics, and Policy                                              3 cr

             the most important ideas in science. This second course will build on principles from the first course
             and investigate key ideas in the chemical and life sciences. Emphasis will be placed on applications
             and interdisciplinary connections across areas of study in the course sequence.
